Main observations from cost_model.py:

1. `batch_metrics(reqs)`:
   - `S_max` = max of align(prompt_len, 64) across the requests in the batch.
   - `G_max` = max of gen_len across the requests in the batch.
   - `cost_prefill` = sum of per-request prefill cost (based on each request's own aligned
 S — not S_max!). Note: prefill cost is per-request, using each request's own aligned
 prompt length.
   - `cost_decode` = len(reqs) * sum_cost_for_g(G_max). Where sum_cost_for_g(n) =
 A * sum_sq_arith(S_max, n) + B*(n*S_max + n(n-1)/2), with A = Kd_attn = 1.0, B = K
d_mlp*HIDDEN = 0.5*4096 = 2048.
     - sum_sq_arith(a, n) = n*a² + a*n(n-1) + n(n-1)(2n-1)/6.
     - So cost_decode_per_req ≈ A*(n*a² + a*n(n-1) + n²²... let me expand: n*a² + a*n
*(n-1) + n(n-1)(2n-1)/6 ≈ n a² + a n² + (2n³)/6 = n a² + a n² + n³/3.
     - cost_decode = N_reqs * (n a² + a n² + n³/3 + B(n a + n²/2)).

     So in a batch with N requests and G_max = g, S_max = s, the decode cost scales
 as N * g * s² + N * g² * s + N * g³/3 + ... This is a big deal: batching together
 requests with a large G_max multiplies all requests' decode cost by g steps. And batching requests
 with large prompts forces all requests to decode against s (S_max) context.

   - Latency: per-request lat = prefill_lat(own S) + lat_decode(G_max, S_max) + batch overhead 
8ms. All requests in the batch get the same lat_decode (max G_max, S_max).
   - pad: pad_prefill = sum(align(own prompt) - prompt) per request (this is fixed regardless of batching —
 align is per-request granularity 64). pad_decode = sum(G_max - gen_len) — depends
 on batching (grouping by similar gen_len reduces padding).
   - real_tokens = sum(prompt+gen).

2. `plan_metrics`:
   - Total cost = sum of batch costs + compile cost + Kbatch_overhead_cost (1e7) * num_batches.
   - Compile cost per unique shape s: 500*s² + 2*s*4096. For s = 64: 500*4096 + 512
00 = 2,048,000 + 51,200 ≈ 2.099e6. For s=2560: 500*6.55e6 + 2*2560*4096 = 3.2768e9 + 2.1e7 ≈ 3.3
e9. Huge for large s.
   - Latency: total latency = each request's latency + Tbatch_overhead_ms (8) per request
. Wait: `lats = [x + self.c.Tbatch_overhead_ms for x in lats]` — this adds 8ms to
 each request's latency. Then Tshape_compile_ms = 1500ms is added to the first request (
smallest batch_id) of each shape.
   - p95 = 95th percentile of all latencies (k = ceil(0.95*800)-1 = ceil(760)-1 = 759, i.e., the 760th smallest out of 800).

   - sequential_timecost = sum of max latency per batch. Note: batch execution time = max
 lat within batch (each batch includes +8ms and possibly +1500ms compile for the first batch of
 a shape).
   - unique_shapes: derived from the plan's shape entries (seq_align, heads_align,
 hidden_align). Max 8 shapes across both buckets — note "up to 8 unique shapes across both
 buckets". So the union of shapes in both plans must be at most 8.

3. Constraints:
   - Each request exactly once.
   - Each batch has identical shapes; seq_align >= ceil(prompt_len/64)*64 (i.e., seq_align
 is a multiple of 64 and >= aligned prompt of the largest prompt in the batch... actually >=
 each request's ceil(prompt/64)*64, so effectively >= align(max_prompt, 64)).
   - Heads=32, hidden=4096.
   - Max 8 unique shapes across both buckets.
   - 1 record per request_id.

Key insight: the dominant variable terms:
- Decode cost in a batch: N * (g s² + s g² + g³/3 + 2048 g s + 1024 g²).
- Batch overhead: 1e7 * N_batches.
- Compile: 500 s² + 8192 s per unique shape.

_batches + sum_shape (500 s² + 8192 s).

Note the term g s²: for large s (long prompts), we want small g. s g²: for large g
, we want small s. This suggests sorting into batches by prompt length AND gen length —
 like 2D bin packing. Requests with long prompts AND long gens are the expensive ones

 long-prompt requests with long-gen is expensive.

Actually since the thresholds for cost/p95/sequential are per bucket (each plan scored
 separately with its own cost model on its own requests), the "across both buckets" constraint
 is only the shape count: total unique seq_align across both plans ≤ 8.

Cost is additive, so: minimize cost_b1 + ... no, each has its own threshold. Minimize each
 bucket's cost independently, subject to shared shape set S (|S| ≤ 8), s ∈ S being a valid shape
 for its bucket.

Approach: for each bucket, given a set of allowed levels S (8 values), the optimal assignment:
 each request goes to a level in S that's ≥ its aligned prompt, and batching within
 a level... let me think about what determines the cost more precisely.

Given a batch with members, s = align(max prompt in batch), g = max gen in batch:

cost_batch = sum_r prefill_cost(r) [fixed] + N*(g s² + s g² + g(g-1)(2g-1)/6 + 204
8 g s + 1024 g(g-1)) + 1e7.

Let me define D(s, g) = g*s² + s*g² + g(g-1)(2g-1)/6 + 2048*g*s + 1024*g*(g-1).

Batch cost (variable part) = N * D(s, g) + 1e7, where N = batch size, s, g = maxima.


Note: D is increasing in s and g. For requests in a level-s group, batching a request with gen
 g_r together with someone with gen G ≥ g_r makes it pay D(s, G) instead of D(s, g_r)
 — worse. So within a level, we want to batch requests with similar gens together:
 sort by gen, split into chunks. Chunk cost = N * D(s, g_max_chunk) + 1e7.

So within each level, we have requests with aligned prompts ≤ s (well, the level assigns the s
 bucket; but S_max of a batch formed only from requests with aligned prompt < s would be < s —
 which is actually better! If a batch doesn't contain any request with aligned prompt = s,
 its actual S_max is lower).

Key realization: the "level" is just a device to limit the number of distinct S_max values
. A batch's actual S_max is the max aligned prompt of its members. So the plan is: choose
 a set A of at most 8 aligned values (multiples of 64); partition each bucket's requests into
 batches; each batch's max aligned prompt ∈ A.

Since cost is increasing in s, for each request, the cheapest is to batch it with requests
 of similar aligned prompt and similar gen. With 800 requests and only up to 8 S_max values,
 each S_max value will cover a range of prompt lengths.

Clean approach:
- Choose 8 levels. For each level l, take all requests whose aligned prompt is in (
prev_level, l], and force... no wait. A request with aligned prompt a can be assigned to
 any batch whose S_max ≥ a, as long as S_max ∈ A. The batch's S_max = max member's a.
 If we form a batch from requests all with a ≤ l and including one with a = l, S_max = l
 ∈ A. Good.

So: sort requests by a = aligned prompt. Partition the sorted list into at most 8 contiguous "
segments" by a (since within a segment, S_max = a of segment's max). But we could also do better
: we might want to split a segment by gen. E.g., requests with a in [1000, 2000] and
 gen in [0,50] → batch with S_max ~1500; requests with a in [1000,2000] and gen in [
50,200] → separate batch, S_max ~1800 (which might be a distinct value). The number
 of distinct S_max values is the count of distinct "max a in a batch" across all batches ≤
 8.

So really, we can have many batches, but only ≤ 8 distinct S_max values. Within each S_max
 value v, we can have many batches (grouped by gen), and each batch's max member a must equal v (at least
 one member has a = v, and all have a ≤ v).

Optimal structure: for each distinct a value in the data, assign it to a "host" level v
 ≥ a, v ∈ A. Then batches: group requests by (host level, gen bucket). Each gen bucket batch
 has S_max = v only if it contains a request with a = v. Requests with a < v in that batch
 don't contribute to S_max. But if a gen bucket at level v has no request with a = v
, then S_max < v — which could create a new distinct S_max value not in A... that
's fine as long as total distinct S_max values ≤ 8. Hmm, actually the constraint is just
 total distinct S_max across all batches ≤ 8. So any value that becomes a batch's max a
 counts.

Simplify: choose A = set of at most 8 levels. Assign each request r to a level v ∈ A,
 v ≥ a_r. Form batches by grouping requests of the same (v, gen bucket) together,
 and make sure each (v, gen bucket) group with a_r < v... gets its actual S_max determined by members. To
 keep the distinct S_max count controlled, just: for each level v and gen bucket b
, batch = all requests assigned to (v, b). S_max = max a in batch. If that max a < v
, the actual S_max is that value. That's a distinct value; could exceed 8.

Alternative cleaner approach: instead of "hosting" requests at a level higher than their a
, we can simply partition by contiguous ranges of a into at most 8 groups; group i
 has S_max = a of its max request. Within group i, batch by gen. This gives distinct
 S_max values ≤ 8, each = group max. But is "group i = contiguous a range" optimal?
 A request with small a could be put in a high group to join with similar gens (avoiding a
 separate small batch) — e.g., a=64, gen=500: putting it in the largest-prompt group (
s=2048) costs it D(2048, g) which is huge. Better to have its own small batch (S_max=6
4). So generally, small-a requests should go to small s.

The only reason to "raise" a request to a higher level is if it's cheaper than making a new batch: raising to a
 higher s increases D(s, g) ~ s*g² + g*s²... vs. new batch cost 1e7 + small s. A new batch
 of size 1 at s=64: cost = D(64, g) + 1e7 ≈ g*4096 + 64*g² + g³/3 + ... for g=100:
 409,600 + 640,000 + 333,000 + 2048*100*64=13.1M... let me compute: 2048*100*64 =
 13,107,200. 1024*g*(g-1) ≈ 10.1M. Total ≈ 25.6M. So a solo batch costs ~25.6M + 1
e7 = 35.6M. If we raise to s=2048 in an existing batch with G_max=200: marginal D
(2048,200) = 200*4.19e6 + 2048*4e4 + 8e6 + 2048*200*2048 + 1024*39800 ≈ 8.4e8 + 8.2e7
 + 8e6 + 8.4e8 + 4.1e7 ≈ 1.76e9. So raising is way more expensive. So: don't raise; keep requests at the smallest possible s.

So the structure is essentially: contiguous ranges of a, at most 8 ranges, within each
 range batch by gen. And the cost per bucket ≈ sum over ranges [sum over gen buckets (
N * D(s_range, g_bucket_max) + 1e7)] + compile(sum over range s: 500 s² + 8192 s).

Also, the number of batches per range (gen buckets) affects the 1e7 overhead; fewer
 batches better, but wider gen range → higher G_max → higher D.

 1.2288 g².

Main observations from cost_model.py:

1. `batch_metrics(reqs)`:
   - `S_max` = max of align(prompt_len, 64) across the requests in the batch.
   - `G_max` = max of gen_len across the requests in the batch.
   - `cost_prefill` = sum of per-request prefill cost (based on each request's own aligned S — not S_max!). Note: prefill cost is per-request, using each request's own aligned prompt length.
   - `cost_decode` = len(reqs) * sum_cost_for_g(G_max). Where sum_cost_for_g(n) = A * sum_sq_arith(S_max, n) + B*(n*S_max + n(n-1)/2), with A = Kd_attn = 1.0, B = Kd_mlp*HIDDEN = 0.5*4096 = 2048.
     - sum_sq_arith(a, n) = n*a² + a*n(n-1) + n(n-1)(2n-1)/6.
     - So cost_decode_per_req ≈ A*(n*a² + a*n(n-1) + n²²... let me expand: n*a² + a*n*(n-1) + n(n-1)(2n-1)/6 ≈ n a² + a n² + (2n³)/6 = n a² + a n² + n³/3.
     - cost_decode = N_reqs * (n a² + a n² + n³/3 + B(n a + n²/2)).

     So in a batch with N requests and G_max = g, S_max = s, the decode cost scales as N * g * s² + N * g² * s + N * g³/3 + ... This is a big deal: batching together requests with a large G_max multiplies all requests' decode cost by g steps. And batching requests with large prompts forces all requests to decode against s (S_max) context.

   - Latency: per-request lat = prefill_lat(own S) + lat_decode(G_max, S_max) + batch overhead 8ms. All requests in the batch get the same lat_decode (max G_max, S_max).
   - pad: pad_prefill = sum(align(own prompt) - prompt) per request (this is fixed regardless of batching — align is per-request granularity 64). pad_decode = sum(G_max - gen_len) — depends on batching (grouping by similar gen_len reduces padding).
   - real_tokens = sum(prompt+gen).

2. `plan_metrics`:
   - Total cost = sum of batch costs + compile cost + Kbatch_overhead_cost (1e7) * num_batches.
   - Compile cost per unique shape s: 500*s² + 2*s*4096. For s = 64: 500*4096 + 51200 = 2,048,000 + 51,200 ≈ 2.099e6. For s=2560: 500*6.55e6 + 2*2560*4096 = 3.2768e9 + 2.1e7 ≈ 3.3e9. Huge for large s.
   - Latency: total latency = each request's latency + Tbatch_overhead_ms (8) per request. Wait: `lats = [x + self.c.Tbatch_overhead_ms for x in lats]` — this adds 8ms to each request's latency. Then Tshape_compile_ms = 1500ms is added to the first request (smallest batch_id) of each shape.
   - p95 = 95th percentile of all latencies (k = ceil(0.95*800)-1 = ceil(760)-1 = 759, i.e., the 760th smallest out of 800).
   - sequential_timecost = sum of max latency per batch. Note: batch execution time = max lat within batch (each batch includes +8ms and possibly +1500ms compile for the first batch of a shape).
   - unique_shapes: derived from the plan's shape entries (seq_align, heads_align, hidden_align). Max 8 shapes across both buckets — note "up to 8 unique shapes across both buckets". So the union of shapes in both plans must be at most 8.

3. Constraints:
   - Each request exactly once.
   - Each batch has identical shapes; seq_align >= ceil(prompt_len/64)*64 (i.e., seq_align is a multiple of 64 and >= aligned prompt of the largest prompt in the batch... actually >= each request's ceil(prompt/64)*64, so effectively >= align(max_prompt, 64)).

Key insight: the dominant variable terms:
- Decode cost in a batch: N * (g s² + s g² + g³/3 + 2048 g s + 1024 g²).
- Batch overhead: 1e7 * N_batches.
- Compile: 500 s² + 8192 s per unique shape.

So we want to group requests to minimize sum of N*(g s² + s g² + g³/3 + ...) + 1e7*n_batches + sum_shape (500 s² + 8192 s).

Note the term g s²: for large s (long prompts), we want small g. s g²: for large g, we want small s. This suggests sorting into batches by prompt length AND gen length — like 2D bin packing. Requests with long prompts AND long gens are the expensive ones; grouping long-gen requests with short-prompt batches is cheap (s small). Grouping long-prompt requests with long-gen is expensive.

The ideal is: sort requests by prompt length; within a prompt-length window, batch requests with similar gen lengths together.

Approach: for each bucket, given a set of allowed levels S (8 values), the optimal assignment: each request goes to a level in S that's ≥ its aligned prompt, and batching within a level... let me think about what determines the cost more precisely.

Given a batch with members, s = align(max prompt in batch), g = max gen in batch:
cost_batch = sum_r prefill_cost(r) [fixed] + N*(g s² + s g² + g(g-1)(2g-1)/6 + 2048 g s + 1024 g(g-1)) + 1e7.

Let me define D(s, g) = g*s² + s*g² + g(g-1)(2g-1)/6 + 2048*g*s + 1024*g*(g-1).

Batch cost (variable part) = N * D(s, g) + 1e7, where N = batch size, s, g = maxima.

Note: D is increasing in s and g. For requests in a level-s group, batching a request with gen g_r together with someone with gen G ≥ g_r makes it pay D(s, G) instead of D(s, g_r) — worse. So within a level, we want to batch requests with similar gens together: sort by gen, split into chunks. Chunk cost = N * D(s, g_max_chunk) + 1e7.

So within each level, we have requests with aligned prompts ≤ s (well, the level assigns the s bucket; but S_max of a batch formed only from requests with aligned prompt < s would be < s — which is actually better! If a batch doesn't contain any request with aligned prompt = s, its actual S_max is lower).

Key realization: the "level" is just a device to limit the number of distinct S_max values. A batch's actual S_max is the max aligned prompt of its members. So the plan is: choose a set A of at most 8 aligned values (multiples of 64); partition each bucket's requests into batches; each batch's max aligned prompt ∈ A.

Since cost is increasing in s, for each request, the cheapest is to batch it with requests of similar aligned prompt and similar gen. With 800 requests and only up to 8 S_max values, each S_max value will cover a range of prompt lengths.

Clean approach:
- Choose 8 levels. For each level l, take all requests whose aligned prompt is in (prev_level, l], and force... no wait. A request with aligned prompt a can be assigned to any batch whose S_max ≥ a, as long as S_max ∈ A. The batch's S_max = max member's a. If we form a batch from requests all with a ≤ l and including one with a = l, S_max = l ∈ A. Good.

So: sort requests by a = aligned prompt. Partition the sorted list into at most 8 contiguous "segments" by a (since within a segment, S_max = a of segment's max). But we could also do better: we might want to split a segment by gen. E.g., requests with a in [1000, 2000] and gen in [0,50] → batch with S_max ~1500; requests with a in [1000,2000] and gen in [50,200] → separate batch, S_max ~1800 (which might be a distinct value). The number of distinct S_max values is the count of distinct "max a in a batch" across all batches ≤ 8.

So really, we can have many batches, but only ≤ 8 distinct S_max values. Within each S_max value v, we can have many batches (grouped by gen), and each batch's max member a must equal v (at least one member has a = v, and all have a ≤ v).

Optimal structure: for each distinct a value in the data, assign it to a "host" level v ≥ a, v ∈ A. Then batches: group requests by (host level, gen bucket). Each gen bucket batch has S_max = v only if it contains a request with a = v. Requests with a < v in that batch don't contribute to S_max. But if a gen bucket at level v has no request with a = v, then S_max < v — which could create a new distinct S_max value not in A... that's fine as long as total distinct S_max values ≤ 8. Hmm, actually the constraint is just total distinct S_max across all batches ≤ 8. So any value that becomes a batch's max a counts.

Simplify: choose A = set of at most 8 levels. Assign each request r to a level v ∈ A, v ≥ a_r. Form batches by grouping requests of the same (v, gen bucket) together, and make sure each (v, gen bucket) group with a_r < v... gets its actual S_max determined by members. To keep the distinct S_max count controlled, just: for each level v and gen bucket b, batch = all requests assigned to (v, b). S_max = max a in batch. If that max a < v, the actual S_max is that value. That's a distinct value; could exceed 8.

Alternative cleaner approach: instead of "hosting" requests at a level higher than their a, we can simply partition by contiguous ranges of a into at most 8 groups; group i has S_max = a of its max request. Within group i, batch by gen. This gives distinct S_max values ≤ 8, each = group max. But is "group i = contiguous a range" optimal? A request with small a could be put in a high group to join with similar gens (avoiding a separate small batch) — e.g., a=64, gen=500: putting it in the largest-prompt group (s=2048) costs it D(2048, g) which is huge. Better to have its own small batch (S_max=64). So generally, small-a requests should go to small s.

The only reason to "raise" a request to a higher level is if it's cheaper than making a new batch: raising to a higher s increases D(s, g) ~ s*g² + g*s²... vs. new batch cost 1e7 + small s. A new batch of size 1 at s=64: cost = D(64, g) + 1e7 ≈ g*4096 + 64*g² + g³/3 + ... for g=100: 409,600 + 640,000 + 333,000 + 2048*100*64=13.1M... let me compute: 2048*100*64 = 13,107,200. 1024*g*(g-1) ≈ 10.1M. Total ≈ 25.6M. So a solo batch costs ~25.6M + 1e7 = 35.6M. If we raise to s=2048 in an existing batch with G_max=200: marginal D(2048,200) = 200*4.19e6 + 2048*4e4 + 8e6 + 2048*200*2048 + 1024*39800 ≈ 8.4e8 + 8.2e7 + 8e6 + 8.4e8 + 4.1e7 ≈ 1.76e9. So raising is way more expensive. So: don't raise; keep requests at the smallest possible s.

So the structure is essentially: contiguous ranges of a, at most 8 ranges, within each range batch by gen. And the cost per bucket ≈ sum over ranges [sum over gen buckets (N * D(s_range, g_bucket_max) + 1e7)] + compile(sum over range s: 500 s² + 8192 s).

Also, the number of batches per range (gen buckets) affects the 1e7 overhead; fewer batches better, but wider gen range → higher G_max → higher D.

 requests: 2*D(s,g) vs D(s,g)+D(s,g) — same. For two different: 2*D(smax,gmax) - D(s
1,g1)-D(s2,g2) = waste. For s1=64,g1=100 and s2=64,g2=102: 2*D(64,102) - D(64,100)-

1)(2g-1)/6 ≈ g³/3 term: D has a g³/3 term (A=1): for g~100: g³/3=3.3e5 vs g s² for
 s=64: 100*4096=4e5. Hmm, comparable.

Decision: singletons are cost-optimal, except we save 1e7 per merged pair, at the cost of
 decode waste. Let me compute the decode waste for merging vs the 1e7 saving: for two requests with (s1,g1),(

So the algorithm:
- Sort requests by (tier s, g).
- Within a tier s, sort by g; partition into groups with small g spread (each group's pad = sum(Gmax - g) fits in a per-group budget), and also keep group latency = lp_max + D_lat(s, Gmax) + 8 ≤ threshold (with margin) so as not to break p95 for the 760.
- Number of batches: as few as possible subject to pad ≤ budget (each merge saves 1e7 cost; but large Gmax groups create pad). Actually, minimizing batches under the pad budget: group greedily: within a tier, sort by g, extend current group while sum pad would be... we want to minimize total batches: largest groups possible. A group of k requests g1≤...≤gk, Gmax=gk, pad = k*Gmax - sum g = sum (gk - gi). To maximize k under pad budget P: take consecutive runs from the top: include requests while the cumulative (gk - gi) ≤ P. But P is global across all tiers (total decode pad ≤ 2838 for b1). Distribute P across tiers to minimize total batches: fewer groups = fewer 1e7. Groups with big s are more valuable to merge (decode waste of a batch is n*(D(smax,gmax)-D(si,gi)) — merging at big s has bigger waste per unit pad, but the 1e7 saving is the same). Cost view: each merge saves 1e7 - waste. Waste ≈ n * ΔD (per-request dD ~ s²+sg+g² scale... per pad unit: ΔD per pad unit ≈ dD/dg * (1 per request pad) ≈ (s² + s g + g²)/... per request dD/dg = s² + s g + g(g-1)... ≈ for s=128,g=100: 16384+12800+9900≈3.9e4 per pad token for that request. A merge at tier 128 with 20 pad tokens waste ≈ 20*3.9e4*... per request... hmm, waste per batch of group size k with pad P_g: ≈ P_g * dD/dg_avg (roughly, since each unit of pad is one request paying dD/dg extra). 20 pad * 4e4 = 8e5 < 1e7 → still saves. Even at s=2048, g=1000: dD/dg = s²+s g+g² ≈ 4.19e6+2.05e6+1e6=7.2e6; 20 pad → 1.4e8 > 1e7 → merging at the top tier costs! So the tier size matters: big s → avoid merging (or only merge requests with nearly identical g, where pad is small but waste still... if two requests have same g: pad=0, waste = n*D(smax,g)-sum: for same g, s1<s2: waste = D(s2,g)-D(s1,g) ≈ g*(s2²-s1²) + g(g-1)(s2-s1) ≈ for s1=1024,s2=2048,g=100: 100*(4.19e6-1.05e6)+1e4*1024≈3.1e8+1e7 ≈ 3.2e8 >> 1e7. Don't merge different s's! Same s: waste 0, pad = k*Gmax - sum g. So: same-tier same-g (or nearly) batches are great: zero waste, small pad, save 1e7 per merge.

So optimal: within each tier s, group by gen proximity: for each gen value g, all requests with (s,g): batch them together (pad 0, waste 0)! **Bucket 1:**
- Minimum total cost (everything solo + 800 overheads + compilation) = 2.8911e11 < 3.0e11 threshold. In other words, cost is achievable with about 1e9 to spare (400M of overhead + ~210M of compilation can be used: if batches are 2 or fewer requests, the overhead stays under 400M... actually the minimum of solo = 800 overheads of 8e9 + compilation ~2.1e9 = 2.8911e11. For the threshold of 3.0e11, we can add up to ~1.09e10 in additional overhead, i.e. up to ~1090 additional batches... but the minimum is already 800 batches. Hmm: total = prefill + decode_solo + 1e7*(#batches) + compile. With #batches=800: 2.8911e11. If we merge 2 requests into 1 batch, we save 1e7 in overhead, but the decode increases by the excess over the max g. The cost threshold is very tight: there's a total of 1.09e10 of slack above the "all solo" lower bound.

Wait, but can we actually merge at all? Merging increases the decode cost by N*D(s,g_max) - sum D(s,g_i) (the excess of the smaller requests up to g_max). Merging 2 requests of g1<g2 costs the excess D_excess(s, g2-g1) = D_lat... no, D_cost(s,g2) - D_cost(s,g1) on the smaller request side. Merging saves 1e7 in overhead. So merging is favorable only when the excess < 1e7. The excess D_cost(s, g, from g1 to g2): for s=64, A*(Δ·g²·... let me calculate: D(s,g) is roughly A*(g·s² + s·g² + g³/3) + B·g·s. For s=64: g·s²=4096g, s·g²=64g², g³/3. For g=100: 409600 + 640000 + 333333 ≈ 1.38e6 + B·6400=1.31e7 → total ~1.45e7? Hmm, that's larger than 1e7. So even merging two identical g's in the s=64 tier costs ~0 excess but saves 1e7 → favorable! Merging identical gen lengths costs zero excess. Merging different g's costs excess > 1e7 → unfavorable.

So the cost-optimal plan for bucket 1: group by (tier, exact g) as much as possible. The overhead for distinct (tier, g) groups: 800 requests, 225 distinct g's total, but per tier... the overhead per batch is 1e7. If each tier-g group becomes 1 batch, the number of batches is ~ total number of distinct (tier,g) pairs. Let me count. If that's ~400-500, cost = 2.295e9 + 2.767e11 + 500e7 + compilation ≈ 2.86e11 ✓.

But sequential: sum of max L per batch. For tier-g groups, max L = lp(s)+D(s,g)+8 (intra-batch variation is small). The sum over all groups is ≈ sum Lmin + (variation within a group, ~0). From the previous calculation, sum Lmin = 3.35e8 > threshold of 2.7e8! That is, even with perfect grouping, the sequential time exceeds the threshold. We need to merge to reduce the sequential time.

Sequential time = sum of max latency per batch. To reduce this, we put multiple requests into the same batch: the batch time = max L within the batch. If 2 requests with L1>L2 are in the same batch, the contribution to the sum = L1 (not L1+L2). The saved amount = L2, but the cost penalty = the excess decode cost + possibly padding.

So for bucket 1's sequential: total 3.35e8, need to reduce by 6.5e7 (24%). Merge pairs: each merge saves ~ the smaller L (~2e5 average) → need ~325 merges, i.e. about 475 batches. But each merge with different g costs excess in decode. Excess of merging g1<g2 into batch with g_max=g2: for small s, ~1.4e7 or more > 1e7 saving?? No wait — the 1e7 saving only applies to the batch overhead of the batch that was eliminated. Each merge reduces #batches by 1, saves 1e7, and adds excess E. Favorable for cost when E < 1e7.

For s=64: excess between g and g' (g<g'): D(64,g')-D(64,g). For g=16, g'=100: D(64,100)-D(64,16). D(64,g) = A*(g·4096 + 64g² + g³/3) + 2048·64g... wait B=2048, sum_lin = g·s + g(g-1)/2 ≈ 64g + g²/2. Decode pad = sum over batches of (N*G - sum(g)), where G = max gen in the batch. If we
 batch same-g requests together, decode pad for that group = 0. The issue is requests with different
 g within the same tier.

Prefill pad is fixed at 22897 (b1) and 24836 (b2). Real token count: 467926 (b1),
 187073 (b2).

For b1: pad_ratio target 0.055 → total_pad <= 0.055*467926 = 25736. Prefill pad = 22897. So decode pad budget = 25
736 - 22897 = 2839. That's tight!

For b2: pad_ratio target 0.15 → total_pad <= 0.15*187073 = 28061. Prefill pad = 2
4836. Decode pad budget = 28061 - 24836 = 3225. Also tight!

So we need to minimize decode pad. Decode pad for a batch = N*G - sum(g) = sum over each
 request of (G - g_i). If all requests in a batch have the same g, decode pad = 0 for
 that batch.

So the ideal is: batch requests with the same g together (within a tier). But we also have cost
 constraints and p95/seq constraints.

Let me rethink. The cost objective:
- cost = prefill_cost (fixed) + sum over batches of (N*D_cost(s,G)) + compile_cost + B*O
HC
- prefill_cost is fixed (depends only on the request itself).
- sum over batches of N*D_cost(s,G): for a given set of requests, sum of D_cost(s
,G) where G is the batch's max gen. To minimize this, for each request the effective G
 should be as small as possible → same-g batching minimizes.
- OHC per batch: more batches = more overhead. So we want fewer batches, but same-g batching
 creates many batches.

There's a tension:
- Pad wants same-g batching (pad=0 per group).
- Cost: D_cost(s,G) is convex in G, so batching different g together costs more per request than
 same-g. But merging reduces OHC count.
- p95 wants few requests to have high G (since per-batch lat = lp + D_lat(s,G) + 8
, and every request in the batch gets that lat). High-G batches push many requests past
 p95.
- seq = sum over batches of max lat. Fewer batches is better, but each batch's max lat should be small
.

This is a multi-objective problem. Let me think about what dominates.

 S_max = the batch's max aligned prompt. If I mix tiers in one batch, S_max = the
 max of that batch (the largest prompt). Then all requests in the batch get decode
 cost at that larger S_max. That's worse for the smaller-prompt requests. So keeping tiers separate
 is strictly better for decode. Yes, tier separation is correct.

- cost: total <= threshold

But is there any benefit to merging adjacent g values? Merging two g groups (g1 < g2) into one batch with G=g2:
- decode cost increases: the g1 requests now pay D_cost(s, g2) instead of D_cost(s, g1).
- pad increases: (g2 - g1) per g1 request.
- p95: the g1 requests now have higher lat (may push past threshold).
- seq: reduces by one batch's contribution.
- OHC: reduces by 1e7.

Merging reduces OHC by 1e7 but increases decode cost by N1*(D_cost(s,g2)-D_cost(s,g1)) + adds pad. D_cost is roughly quadratic in g (dominated by s^2*g + s*g^2). For s=128, g1=16, g2=20: D_cost(128,16) vs D_cost(128,20). The difference times N1 may be small, and the 1e7 OHC savings may or may not be worth it. But actually, I realized there's an even better structure. Let me reconsider the problem:

Per-tier optimization: partition sorted gens into groups. Decision variables: group boundaries.
- Cost contribution: Σ_groups [N*D(s,G) + 1e7]
- Seq contribution: Σ_groups lat(s,G)
- p95: count of reqs in groups with lat>thr (each group is uniform, so all-or-nothing per group)

Cost is minimized by many groups (N=1 → D(s,g_i)); seq is minimized by few groups (one per tier); p95 wants groups with G that makes lat ≤ thr (i.e. split off high-g tails... no wait, a group's lat depends on its Gmax; a solo high-g request has high lat anyway — unavoidable).

So: find per-tier partition that minimizes cost subject to per-tier Σ lat ≤ tier budget and per-tier exc ≤ tier budget. Allocate total budget 2.7e8 / 3.2e7 and exc 40 across tiers.

DP with two resource constraints (cost, seq) is heavy. Simpler: Lagrangian with per-tier adaptive scaling, or just: for each tier, compute Pareto frontier of (seq_k, cost_k) for partitions with k groups... too complex.

Practical approach: scalarize per-tier: objective = cost + L_t * seq, L_t = (tier's cost when one batch) / (tier's seq when N groups) — normalize so both terms are same magnitude. Then L_t * seq and cost are comparable in each tier. Global p95/pad/seq constraints checked post-hoc; if violated, increase L or add le and re-tune. Since each tier is small (≤276 reqs, 11 tiers), we can try many L per tier and pick per-tier partition that fits global budgets via a small search (e.g. pick per-tier "aggressiveness" level a_t ∈ {0,1,2,4,8,...} as merge budget).

Merging into a batch with G_new ≥ G_old: each moved req i's decode changes from D(s, G_old_batch) to D(s, G_new). Δcost = -1e7 + Σ_i [D(s,G_new) - D(s,G_old_i)].

Δseq = -lat_small_batch (roughly, for merging the small batch into the big).

So the merge trade-off: gain 1e7 overhead + save lat_small in seq; pay Σ_i [D(s,G_new)-D(s,G_old_i)].

OK, enough analysis — let me implement the exact per-tier DP with the monster/safe constraint and run a 2D search over (L, le, c2) to find a point satisfying all constraints. Since tiers are small, per-tier DP is fast (n ≤ 276, O(n²) per tier → ~3e4 per tier, ~1e5 per bucket — trivial).

Implementation:
Per tier s, sorted gens g[0..n-1], Gcap = max G such that lat(s,G) ≤ thr (binary search; if lat(s,g_min) > thr, Gcap = -1 meaning all monsters).
DP[i] = min over valid j of: dp[j] + N*D(s,G) + 1e7 + L*(lp+Dlat+8) + le*N*(1[G>thr-safe...])...

Actually, the exc term: groups with lat > thr contribute N exc. In valid groups: safe groups (G ≤ Gcap) → 0 exc; monster groups (all g > Gcap) → N exc. Note: Gcap is defined by lat ≤ thr, so monster groups (j..i-1 with all g > Gcap) have G > Gcap → lat > thr → exc = N. Correct.

DP validity: group j..i-1 valid if g[i-1] ≤ Gcap (safe: all members ≤ Gcap since sorted) or g[j] > Gcap (monster: all > Gcap).

Objective per group: N*D(s,G) + 1e7 + L*lat + le*N*I(monster).
Also add c2*pad: c2*(N*G - (pref[i]-pref[j])).

Then reconstruct; compute true metrics; check global constraints. Search L over [1,10,30,100,300,1e3,3e3,1e4,3e4,1e5,3e5,1e6], le over [0, 1e3, 1e4, 1e5], c2 over [0, 1e2, 1e3] (pad weight; pad is already tight, we need c2 large enough to respect the budget — b1 dec_pad budget 2839; solo has dec_pad=0; merges add pad; cost L already pushes toward few merges... pad might need its own weight).

Now, is the contiguous grouping in g-sorted order optimal within a tier? For a fixed
 tier s, we want to partition the multiset of g's into batches. Each batch's cost depends only on its G_max

 g's assigned to a batch, D(s,G_max) is determined by the max. To minimize total, we
 want to group requests with similar g's (so G_max doesn't overshoot much), which is achieved by
 sorting and grouping contiguously. This is a standard result: for a cost function that's
 monotone in the batch's max, the optimal partition into contiguous groups on the sorted array
. Actually, is it provably optimal that batches are contiguous? Consider g's {1,

11}+{10} is dominated by {1,10}+{11} (same or better). So contiguous is fine. Good.

Now, is the contiguous grouping in g-sorted order optimal within a tier? For a fixed tier s, we want to partition the multiset of g's into batches. Each batch's cost depends only on its G_max (and N, and sum of g for pad). For decode cost: N*D(s,G_max). For a fixed set of g's assigned to a batch, D(s,G_max) is determined by the max. To minimize total, we want to group requests with similar g's (so G_max doesn't overshoot much), which is achieved by sorting and grouping contiguously. This is a standard result: for a cost function that's monotone in the batch's max, the optimal partition into contiguous groups on the sorted array. Actually, is it provably optimal that batches are contiguous? Consider g's {1, 10, 11}. Batches {1,11} and {10} vs {1,10} and {11}. Cost is D(max). Option A: D(11)+D(10), pad = (11-1)+(10-10)=10. Option B: D(10)+D(11), pad=(10-1)+0=9. Same cost, B has less pad. So contiguous (in g) grouping {1,10},{11}... hmm, {1,11} and {10} isn't contiguous. Contiguous options: {1},{10,11} or {1,10},{11} or {1,10,11} or {1},{10},{11}. The DP over contiguous groups covers {1,10}+{11} and {1}+{10,11}. The non-contiguous {1,11}+{10} is dominated by {1,10}+{11} (same or better). So contiguous is fine. Good.

Wait, the real problem is: the tier is processed as a sorted list of generators. flags
 correspond to the original order of reqs, and are mapped through order. But in solve
_allg I build tiers[s].append(gen) and tierflag[s].append(...) in the same request
 order, so they align. In tier_dp, `order=list(range(len(gens)))` — the identity map! I'm
 not sorting the gens in order (I use order to index, but since order is the identity
, g = gens as is, unsorted!). So the sortedness assumption is broken: g might not be sorted, the contiguity-optimal property still structurally
 holds, but "safe or g[j]>cap" becomes wrong: for an unsorted list, a group ending at
 i-1 with G=g[i-1]: the members have various g values, some of which may be > cap even though
 G <= cap. The check `safe or g[j]>cap` assumes all members j..i-1 have g <= G... which is only true if sorted. In my old dp2 I sorted with g=sorted(gens) — no problem. In search2 I dropped the
 sort (to pair flags with gens). Fix: build pairs (gen, flag), sort by gen, and carry
 flags along.

05 sec) ≈ 400 sec. Plus the first iteration of each config... yeah, ~480 sec. Reduce: drop

The search grid is too large (4×4 cuts × 3 ws × 4 wc × 4 wp × 5 we = 960 configs; each config runs 2 solve_allg's, and each solve includes a DP over 800 elements and verification... each solve is ~O(n²) per tier = fine, but the verification using cost_model's plan_metrics is O(B) batches... 960 × probably 0.5 sec = 480 sec + verification overhead). It timed out at 420 seconds. Let's reduce the grid and run it in the background.

Actually, the issue is: each verify call runs cost_model's plan_metrics, which is O(n²) per batch (sum_sq_arith is fine, but per batch: it computes _sum_sq_arith(a,n) — cheap; lat_prefill is per request; the cost_decode loop is per request... it's a dict operations heavy plan_metrics: batches are defaultdict; 800 requests → fine, probably 50ms each. 960 configs × 2 buckets × (solve ~0.15 sec + verify ~0.05 sec) ≈ 400 sec. Plus the first iteration of each config... yeah, ~480 sec. Reduce: drop we (always 0 — it never mattered), wp ∈ {0, 5, 20}, wc ∈ {0, 2, 5}, ws ∈ {1, 5, 20} → 4×4×3×3×3=432 configs. And run in the background with a progress file.
